● That reframes it correctly. Those 6 aren't missing gRPC services to define —
  the frontend calling them as standalone operation.<domain>.v1.*Service
  packages is the architectural error. The only valid creation path is
  org.dbus.v1.plugins, i.e. they register as plugins and are reached through the
  already-mounted PluginService.CallMethod, not new proto packages. Let me
  confirm where plugins register and which of the 6 domains already have a
  plugin vs. need one created.

● Recorded as OD-30 in SIGNALS and saved the rule to memory
  ([[project_only_valid_path_plugins]]).

  Bottom line on your question: yes — those calls have been silently failing
  because the frontend invented 6 operation.<domain>.v1.*Service packages that
  have no proto and no impl. The correct path is exactly what you said —
  org.dbus.v1.plugins. None become new proto packages:

  - btrfs — zero work; plugin already exists. Just repoint the frontend at
  PluginService.CallMethod{plugin_id="btrfs"}.
  - blockchain — op-blockchain crate exists but isn't in default_registry.rs;
  needs a one-line registry entry + a StatePlugin impl wrapper.
  - embedding — new plugin fronting the Qdrant/Voyage shuttle.
  - stores/DataStore — wrap op-cozo-store/op-state-store.
  - agents/Persona — wrap op-agents/agent_config.
  - accountability — route through existing ctl_plane_chatbot + memory
  projection; it's a projection, not a service.

  Want me to start with the free win (btrfs wiring + register the blockchain
  plugin, since its crate already exists), or scaffold all five as plugin stubs
  in one pass?

  On hardcoding — nothing to convert

  I checked specifically because of your rule. The plugin catalog is already 
  dynamic: available_plugins() (default_registry.rs:364) parses
  include_str!("state_plugins/mod.rs") for pub mod lines and strips the _plugin
  suffix. So adding pub mod blockchain_plugin; is what registered it in the
  catalog — I did not add it to any hardcoded name list. default_auto_load() is
  empty (Vec::new()), so no list there either.

  The one remaining hardcoded element is the load_plugin match arm (string →
  constructor). That's a dispatch table, not a catalog list — it can't collapse
  to a plain data list because constructors differ (some take config paths,
  WgcfConfig, etc.). Making that dynamic means a trait-object
  registration/inventory pattern across all ~70 plugins — a real refactor,
  separate from this task. Want me to take that on next, or move to the next of
  the 6 (the free btrfs frontend rewire)?

❯ ctor = actor?                                                                 

● ctor = constructor (standard C++/Rust shorthand for the function that builds
  an instance) — not "actor". But you're right that it collides visually with
  actor/actor_id (the sled-identity concept in MutationEngine), which would be
  genuinely confusing in this codebase. I'll name the field build instead so
  there's no ambiguity. Proceeding with that.

❯ what is coercion                                                              

❯ how did we go from 70+ to 62

● Done — the central dispatch table is gone; each plugin self-registers next to
  its own definition, and both load_plugin and available_plugins read the
  inventory. Now your two questions:

  What is coercion

  A coercion is an implicit type conversion the Rust compiler inserts
  automatically at certain spots ("coercion sites") so a value of one type can
  be used where a closely-related type is expected — no explicit as cast needed.

  The specific one here is an unsized coercion: Arc<BlockchainPlugin> → Arc<dyn 
  StatePlugin>.
  - BlockchainPlugin is a concrete, fixed-size type.
  - dyn StatePlugin is a trait object — unsized; an Arc to it carries an extra
  vtable pointer.
  - Because BlockchainPlugin implements StatePlugin, the compiler can rewrite
  the pointer to attach that vtable.

  My registration field is typed build: fn(&PluginCtx) -> Arc<dyn StatePlugin>.
  Each closure literally returns Arc::new(BlockchainPlugin::new()), i.e.
  Arc<BlockchainPlugin>. The closure's tail expression is a coercion site, so
  the compiler silently converts it to Arc<dyn StatePlugin>. That's why I didn't
  have to write ... as Arc<dyn StatePlugin> on all 62 — I'd flagged it as a
  risk because closure-to-fn-pointer + unsizing don't always fire together; the
  clean build confirmed it does.

  How 70+ became 62

  "70+" was my loose estimate before I'd actually counted. When I read the whole
  load_plugin match and enumerated it, there were exactly 62 constructor arms
  (61 original + the blockchain one I'd just added). So 62 is the real number —
  the registrations map 1:1 to the arms that existed.

  The reason it looks like more: state_plugins/mod.rs has well over 62 pub mod
  lines, but many aren't plugins — they're helpers (common, plugin_schema_defs,
  schemars_adapter, incus_device, schema_contract) or
  commented-out/unimplemented (// pub use privacy::…, systemd,
  systemd_networkd). The old available_plugins() even had a hardcoded filter
  (is_state_plugin_helper_module) to skip the helpers. The authoritative count
  is the constructors that actually existed: 62. The inventory now is that count
  — no filtering needed, because only real plugins call submit!.

  Want me to commit this (inventory refactor + the blockchain plugin), or move
  on to the next of the 6 frontend services?

❯ what about this:Model Choices                                                 
  Voyage currently provides the following contextualized chunk embedding        
  models:                                                                       
                                                                                
  Model                                                                         
                                                                                
  Per Chunk Context Window                                                      
                                                                                
  Context Length (tokens)                                                       
                                                                                
  Embedding Dimension                                                           
                                                                                
  Description                                                                   
                                                                                
  In Preview:voyage-context-4                                                   
                                                                                
  32,000                                                                        
                                                                                
  120,000*                                                                      
                                                                                
  1024 (default), 256, 512, 2048                                                
                                                                                
  Contextualized chunk embeddings optimized for general-purpose and             
  multilingual retrieval quality.                                               
                                                                                
  voyage-context-3                                                              
                                                                                
  32,000                                                                        
                                                                                
  120,000*                                                                      
                                                                                
  1024 (default), 256, 512, 2048                                                
                                                                                
  Contextualized chunk embeddings optimized for general-purpose and             
  multilingual retrieval quality.                                               
                                                                                
  To learn more, see the blog post.                                             
                                                                                
  Python API                                                                    
  Voyage contextualized chunk embeddings are accessible in Python through the   
  voyageai package. Install the package, set up your API key, and use           
  voyageai.Client.contextualized_embed() to vectorize your inputs.              
                                                                                
  voyageai.Client.contextualized_embed( inputs: Union[List[List[str]],          
  List[str]], # see below for specifics on when to pass one or the other.       
  model: str, input_type: Optional[str] = None, output_dimension: Optional[int] 
  = None, output_dtype: Optional[str] = "float", enable_auto_chunking:          
  Optional[bool] = False, chunk_size: Optional[int] = 512, chunk_overlap:       
  Optional[int] = 0, chunk_fn: Optional[Callable[[str], List[str]]] = None, )   
                                                                                
  Parameters                                                                    
                                                                                
  inputs (List[List[str]] or List[str]) - The input texts to be vectorized.     
  model (str) - Name of the model. Recommended options: voyage-context-4.       
  input_type (str, optional, defaults to None) - Type of the input text.        
  Options: None, query, document.                                               
  When input_type is None, the model directly converts the inputs into          
  numerical vectors. For retrieval and search, we recommend setting input_type  
  to query or document. In those cases, Voyage automatically prepends a prompt  
  before vectorizing the input. Embeddings generated with and without           
  input_type are compatible.                                                    
  output_dimension (int, optional, defaults to None) - The number of dimensions 
  for resulting embeddings.                                                     
  Options: 2048, 1024 (default), 512, and 256.                                  
  output_dtype The data type for returned embeddings.                           
  Options: float, int8, uint8, binary, ubinary. See Flexible Dimensions and     
  Quantization for details.                                                     
  float: Each embedding is a list of 32-bit floating-point numbers.             
  int8 and uint8: Each embedding is a list of 8-bit integers.                   
  binary and ubinary: Each embedding is a list of 8-bit integers representing   
  bit-packed single-bit values. The returned list length is 1/8 of              
  output_dimension.binaryuses offset binary.                                    
  enable_auto_chunking (bool, optional, defaults to False) - Whether to         
  automatically chunk each input document on the backend. When True, inputs     
  must be a flat List[str] of full-document strings, and input_type must be     
  document.                                                                     
  chunk_size (int, optional) - Target chunk size in tokens when                 
  enable_auto_chunking=True. If omitted, the server resolves it to 512.         
  chunk_size must not exceed 32K tokens.                                        
  chunk_overlap (int, optional, defaults to 0) - Chunk overlap for improved     
  context across chunks in tokens when enable_auto_chunking=True. chunk_overlap 
  must be smaller than chunk_size. Only a valid input when                      
  enable_auto_chunking=True.                                                    
  🚧                                                                            
  The listed limits for both chunk_size and chunk_overlap are upper bounds. The 
  actual chunk_size and chunk_overlap can be less than the value passed, but    
  cannot be higher.                                                             
  Overlapping tokens are billed in the same way as input tokens.                
  chunk_fn (Callable[[str], List[str]], optional, defaults to None) - A custom  
  client-side chunking function. If provided, it is applied locally to each     
  input string before the request is sent. For convenience,                     
  voyageai.default_chunk_fn is available. Use chunk_fn for client-side chunking 
  only; it cannot be combined with enable_auto_chunking=True.                   
  Returns                                                                       
                                                                                
  A ContextualizedEmbeddingsObject, containing the following attributes:        
  results (List[ContextualizedEmbeddingsResult]) - One result per query or      
  document.                                                                     
  embeddings (List[List[float]] or List[List[int]]) - Embeddings corresponding  
  to a query, a document, or chunks from the same document. For document        
  chunks, embeddings are ordered to match chunk order.                          
  chunk_texts (List[str]) - Chunk text returned by the Python SDK for chunked   
  document results. If you provide a client-side chunk_fn, these correspond to  
  the chunks produced by that function. When enable_auto_chunking=True they     
  correspond to the backend-generated chunks.                                   
  index (int) - The index of the query or document in the input list.           
  total_tokens (int) - The total number of tokens in the input texts.           
                                                                                

● voyage-context-4 is a different family, not a 4th member of the trio. Key
  distinctions:

  - Different API/representation. It's contextualized chunk embeddings —
  contextualized_embed(), where each chunk is embedded with its surrounding 
  document context, returning per-chunk vectors (+ chunk_texts, auto-chunking).
  The trio uses plain embed(). That's a fundamentally different embedding
  representation, not just a different tier.
  - Same 1024 default dim ≠ same space. Dimension match is necessary but not
  sufficient for fusion. Voyage's "all 4-series embeddings are compatible"
  statement covers the voyage-4 embedding models (large/4/lite/nano) — it does
  not claim voyage-context-4 vectors are interchangeable with voyage-4 vectors.
  I won't assert they fuse or don't; the docs you pasted don't establish it. So
  the safe rule: its own collection unless Voyage explicitly states 
  cross-compatibility — dropping context-4 vectors into the voyage-4 collection
  on a dimension-match assumption would silently corrupt retrieval
  ([[feedback_no_unfounded_generalizations]]).
  - context-4 ↔ context-3: likely their own internal pair, but again not stated
  in what you pasted — I won't assume.

  So the picture is now: trio (large/4/lite, one shared space) + several
  separate spaces (nano, code-3 [deprecated], law-2, and the context-* family) —
  each its own collection.

  Where this gets interesting: contextualized chunk embeddings are genuinely
  better for RAG (chunks retain document context), so voyage-context-4 is a
  strong candidate for the corpus/RAG path — but that's a pipeline change
  (chunking + per-chunk vectors + a separate collection), not a drop-in
  VoyageModel variant. For single-unit reasoning episodes (ctl_plane_chatbot)
  the contextual benefit is smaller.

  Two separate questions, and I'd rather not guess:

  1. Do you want to adopt voyage-context-4 for the RAG corpus (new
  contextualized-embed pipeline + its own collection) — that's the real upgrade
  — or just have it catalogued as a known separate-space model?
  2. For the ctl_plane_chatbot reasoning-episode embeddings, keep the plain 
  voyage-4 trio, or also switch those to contextualized?

  Tell me the intent and I'll wire it correctly — including whether to model
  these as a separate VoyageContextModel (own space) vs. extending the existing
  enum.

❯ is this what we are talking abour?:Flexible Dimensions and Quantization       
  Storage and search costs in vector-based search can become significant for    
  large corpora, such as in code retrieval with massive repositories. The costs 
  scale linearly in the embedding dimensionality and precision (i.e., the       
  number of bits used to encode each number). Lower dimensional embeddings and  
  quantization (e.g., binary or int8 representations) are used to dramatically  
  lower costs without losing much retrieval quality. These are enabled by       
  Matryoshka learning and quantization-aware training.                          
                                                                                
  Matryoshka Embeddings                                                         
  Matryoshka learning creates embeddings with a nested family of embeddings     
  with various lengths within a single vector. Concretely, for each k in {256,  
  512, and 1024}, the first k entries of a 2048-dimensional embedding also form 
  a valid k-dimensional embedding that is shorter with a slight loss of         
  retrieval quality. This allows users to vectorize documents into a long       
  2048-dimensional vector in advance and have the flexibility to use shorter    
  versions of the embedding (by taking the first k entries) without re-invoking 
  the embedding model.                                                          
                                                                                
  Newer Voyage models, such as voyage-4-large, generate Matryoshka embeddings   
  and support multiple output dimensions directly through the output_dimension  
  parameter (see supported model embedding dimensions here).                    
                                                                                
  How can I truncate Matryoshka embeddings?                                     
  You can truncate Matryoshka embeddings by keeping the leading subset of       
  dimensions. For example, the following Python code demonstrates how to        
  truncate 1024-dimensional vectors to 256 dimensions:                          
                                                                                
  Python                                                                        
                                                                                
  import voyageai                                                               
  import numpy as np                                                            
                                                                                
  def embd_normalize(v: np.ndarray) -> np.ndarray:                              
      """                                                                       
      Normalize the rows of a 2D NumPy array to unit vectors by dividing each   
  row by its Euclidean                                                          
      norm. Raises a ValueError if any row has a norm of zero to prevent        
  division by zero.                                                             
      """                                                                       
      row_norms = np.linalg.norm(v, axis=1, keepdims=True)                      
      if np.any(row_norms == 0):                                                
          raise ValueError("Cannot normalize rows with a norm of zero.")        
      return v / row_norms                                                      
                                                                                
                                                                                
  vo = voyageai.Client()                                                        
                                                                                
  # Generate voyage-4-large vectors, which by default are 1024-dimensional      
  floating-point numbers                                                        
  embd = vo.embed(["Sample text 1", "Sample text 2"],                           
  model="voyage-4-large").embeddings                                            
                                                                                
  # Set shorter dimension                                                       
  short_dim = 256                                                               
                                                                                
  # Resize and normalize vectors to shorter dimension                           
  resized_embd = embd_normalize(np.array(embd)[:, :short_dim]).tolist()         
  Quantization                                                                  
  Quantized embeddings have lower precision, with 8 bits or 1 bit per           
  dimension, reducing storage costs by 4x or 32x compared to 32-bit floats.     
  Newer Voyage embedding models, such as voyage-4-large, support                
  lower-precision embeddings in various data types: int8 (8-bit signed          
  integer), uint8 (8-bit unsigned integer), binary (bit-packed int8), and       
  ubinary (bit-packed uint8). Most vector databases directly support storing    
  and searching with quantized embeddings, including Milvus, Qdrant, Weaviate,  
  Elasticsearch, and Vespa AI.                                                  
                                                                                
  Supported Voyage models enable quantization by specifying the output data     
  type with the output_dtype parameter:                                         
                                                                                
  float: Each returned embedding is a list of 32-bit (4-byte) single-precision  
  floating-point numbers. This is the default and provides the highest          
  precision / retrieval accuracy.                                               
                                                                                
  int8 and uint8: Each returned embedding is a list of 8-bit (1-byte) integers  
  ranging from -128 to 127 and 0 to 255, respectively.                          
                                                                                
  binary and ubinary: Each returned embedding is a list of 8-bit integers that  
  represent bit-packed, quantized single-bit embedding values: int8 for binary  
  and uint8 for ubinary. The length of the returned list of integers is 1/8 of  
  the actual dimension of the embedding. The binary type uses the offset binary 
  method, explained below.                                                      
                                                                                
  Binary quantization example:                                                  
                                                                                
  Consider the following eight embedding values: -0.03955078, 0.006214142,      
  -0.07446289, -0.039001465, 0.0046463013, 0.00030612946, -0.08496094, and      
  0.03994751. With binary quantization, values less than or equal to zero will  
  be quantized to a binary zero, and positive values to a binary one, resulting 
  in the following binary sequence: 0, 1, 0, 0, 1, 1, 0, 1. These eight bits    
  are then packed into a single 8-bit integer: 01001101 (with the leftmost bit  
  as the most significant bit).                                                 
                                                                                
  ubinary: The binary sequence is directly converted and represented as the     
  unsigned integer (uint8) 77.                                                  
  binary: The binary sequence is represented as the signed integer (int8) -51,  
  calculated using the offset binary method (77 - 128 = -51).                   
  Offset binary                                                                 
  Offset binary is a method for representing negative numbers in binary form    
  (i.e., signed number representations). This approach is used when             
  representing quantized binary embedding values, specifically when the         
  output_dtype parameter is set to binary. The binary values are bit-packed,    
  with each 8-bit sequence represented as an integer calculated using the       
  offset binary method. In this method, an offset is added to an integer before 
  converting to binary and subtracted when converting from binary to a signed   
  integer. For signed 8-bit integers, which have a range of -128 to 127, the    
  offset is typically 128.                                                      
                                                                                
  Signed integer to binary example:                                             
                                                                                
  To represent -32 as an 8-bit binary number:                                   
                                                                                
  Add the offset (128) to -32, resulting in 96.                                 
  Convert 96 to binary: 01100000.                                               
  Binary to signed integer example:                                             
                                                                                
  To determine the signed integer from the 8-bit binary number 01010101:        
                                                                                
  Convert it directly to an integer: 85.                                        
  Subtract the offset (128) from 85, resulting in -43.                          
  Examples                                                                      
  Below are Python code examples for converting and working with binary         
  embeddings.                                                                   
                                                                                
  Convert float to binary & ubinary                                             
  Unpack binary & ubinary embeddings                                            
                                                                                
  import numpy as np                                                            
  import voyageai                                                               
                                                                                
  vo = voyageai.Client()                                                        
                                                                                
  # Generate float embeddings                                                   
  embd_float = vo.embed('Sample text 1', model='voyage-4-large',                
  output_dimension=2048).embeddings[0]                                          
                                                                                
  # Compute 512-dimensional bit-packed binary and ubinary embeddings from       
  2048-dimensional float embeddings                                             
  embd_binary_calc = (np.packbits(np.array(embd_float) > 0, axis=0) -           
  128).astype(np.int8).tolist() # Quantize, binary offset                       
  embd_binary_512_calc = embd_binary_calc[0:64] # Truncate. Binary is 1/8       
  length of embedding dimension.                                                
                                                                                
  embd_ubinary_calc = (np.packbits(np.array(embd_float) > 0,                    
  axis=0)).astype(np.uint8).tolist() # Quantize, binary offset                  
  embd_ubinary_512_calc = embd_ubinary_calc[0:64] # Truncate. Binary is 1/8     
  length of embedding dimension.                                                
  We also have a getting started tutorial in Google Colab here.                 
                                                                                
                                                                                

● Related, but it's a different axis — and yes, it's worth folding into "set up
  correctly." There are actually two independent "no re-vectorize" properties,
  plus a storage one:

  1. Cross-model shared space (what VoyageModel/SHARED_SPACE captures): large ↔
  4 ↔ lite produce comparable vectors. Switch models, no re-index.
  2. Matryoshka (this doc) — within-model dimension nesting: a 2048-dim vector's
  first k entries are themselves a valid {256, 512, 1024}-dim embedding. So you
  embed once at the high dim and truncate (leading-k) to a shorter dim anytime,
  without re-calling Voyage — then re-normalize.
  3. Quantization (also this doc): float → int8 → binary, 4×/32× storage
  reduction; Qdrant supports it natively.

  These compose: cross-model + Matryoshka + quantization are three orthogonal
  knobs.

  How it ties to what we set up:
  - The hard invariant is one collection = one fixed dimension. Matryoshka lets
  you choose that dimension (and even shrink the whole collection later by
  leading-k truncation, no re-embed) — but everything in the collection must
  share it. We pinned SHARED_EMBEDDING_DIMS = 1024, which is the trio's default
  and a valid Matryoshka cut. Correct.
  - This is exactly why voyage-4-nano is out: its dimensions top out at 512
  (default 512; options 128/256) — it can't produce a 1024-dim vector, so it
  can't live in the 1024 collection. The exclusion is dimensional, and
  Matryoshka makes that precise: you'd have to truncate the trio down to 512 to
  ever include nano, degrading the trio. Not worth it.

  So the accurate model of the setup is: trio in one 1024-dim collection, which 
  (thanks to Matryoshka) you can later truncate to 512/256 or quantize to 
  int8/binary for cost — all without re-embedding.

  Right now the config has a single vector_dims: u32 with only a Min(0)
  constraint. To make the setup fully correct per these docs, I'd:
  - constrain vector_dims to the Matryoshka set {256, 512, 1024, 2048} (not an
  arbitrary int), default 1024;
  - add an output_dtype field (float/int8/uint8/binary/ubinary) so quantization
  is a first-class, declared choice;
  - note in the schema that shrinking dim or quantizing is a leading-k/precision
  change, not a re-vectorize.

  Want me to make those two config changes to ctl_plane_chatbot
  (Matryoshka-constrained vector_dims + output_dtype)? That's the concrete "set
  up the vectors correctly" follow-through from this doc.

      142 +/// Matryoshka output dimensions. A 2048-dim voyage-4 vector's
          + leading *k* entries                                          
      143 +/// (256/512/1024) are themselves a valid k-dim embedding, so 
          +a collection can be                                           
      144 +/// truncated to a shorter dim later **without re-vectorizing*
          +* (leading-k +                                                
      145 +/// re-normalize). Every vector in one Qdrant collection must 
          +still share one dim;                                          
      146 +/// 1024 is the trio default.                                 
      147  #[derive(Debug, Clone, Serialize, Deserialize, schemars::JsonS
           chema, PartialEq)]
      148 +pub enum MatryoshkaDim {                                      
      149 +    #[serde(rename = "256")]                                  
      150 +    D256,                                                     
      151 +    #[serde(rename = "512")]                                  
      152 +    D512,                                                     
      153 +    #[serde(rename = "1024")]                                 
      154 +    D1024,                                                    
      155 +    #[serde(rename = "2048")]                                 
      156 +    D2048,                                                    
      157 +}                                                             
      158 +                                                              
      159 +impl Default for MatryoshkaDim {                              
      160 +    fn default() -> Self {                                    
      161 +        MatryoshkaDim::D1024                                  
      162 +    }                                                         
      163 +}                                                             
      164 +                                                              
      165 +impl MatryoshkaDim {                                          
      166 +    /// Numeric dimensionality.                               
      167 +    pub fn dims(&self) -> u32 {                               
      168 +        match self {                                          
      169 +            MatryoshkaDim::D256 => 256,                       
      170 +            MatryoshkaDim::D512 => 512,                       
      171 +            MatryoshkaDim::D1024 => 1024,                     
      172 +            MatryoshkaDim::D2048 => 2048,                     
      173 +        }                                                     
      174 +    }                                                         
      175 +}                                                             
      176 +                                                              
      177 +/// Output precision / quantization for stored vectors. `float
          +` (default, highest                                           
      178 +/// accuracy) → `int8`/`uint8` (4× smaller) → `binary`/`ubinar
          +y` (32× smaller,                                              
      179 +/// bit-packed). Qdrant stores and searches all of these nativ
          +ely. Changing dtype

          +chema, PartialEq)]

      189 +}

❯ we are inline with all of this?:FAQ                                           
  General                                                                       
  Why do Voyage embeddings have superior quality?                               
  Embedding models, much like generative models, rely on powerful neural        
  networks (often transformer-based) to capture and compress semantic context.  
  And, much like generative models, they’re incredibly hard to train. Voyage is 
  a team of leading AI researchers who each have experience in training         
  embedding models for 5+ years. We refine every component, from model          
  architecture and data collection to selecting suitable loss functions and     
  optimizers. Please see our blog for more details.                             
                                                                                
  Model                                                                         
  What text embedding models are available, and which one should I use?         
  For general-purpose embedding, our recommendation is voyage-4-large for the   
  best quality, voyage-4-lite for the lowest latency and cost, and voyage-4 for 
  a balance between the two. Notably, all 4 series models are compatible with   
  each other . voyage-4 also outperforms all major competitors in retrieval     
  quality while being offered at a lower price point. For retrieval, please use 
  the input_type parameter to specify whether the text is a query or document,  
  which adds instructions on the backend.                                       
                                                                                
  If your application is in a domain addressed by one of our domain-specific    
  embedding models, we recommend using that model. Specifically:                
                                                                                
  voyage-law-2 is recommended for retrieval tasks in the legal domain.          
  voyage-code-3 is recommended for code-related tasks and programming           
  documentation.                                                                
  voyage-finance-2 is recommended for finance-related tasks.                    
  Which similarity function should I use?                                       
  You can use Voyage embeddings with either dot-product similarity, cosine      
  similarity, or Euclidean distance. An explanation about embedding similarity  
  can be found here.                                                            
                                                                                
  Voyage AI embeddings are normalized to length 1, which means that:            
                                                                                
  Cosine similarity is equivalent to dot-product similarity, while the latter   
  can be computed more quickly.                                                 
  Cosine similarity and Euclidean distance will result in the identical         
  rankings.                                                                     
  What is the relationship between characters, words, and tokens?               
  Please see this section.                                                      
                                                                                
  When and how should I use the input_type parameter?                           
  For retrieval/search tasks and use cases (e.g., RAG), where a "query" is used 
  to search for relevant information among a collection of data referred to as  
  "documents," we recommend specifying whether your inputs are intended as      
  queries or documents by setting input_type to query or document,              
  respectively. Do not omit input_type or set input_type=None. Specifying       
  whether inputs are qu                                                         
  ──── (132 lines hidden) ───────────────────────────────────────────────────── 
  ceive the bill?                                                               
  The first 200 million tokens are free for every account, and subsequent usage 
  is priced on a per-token basis. See our Pricing page for details              
                                                                                
  You can add payment methods to your account in the dashboard. We will bill    
  monthly. You can expect a credit card charge around the 2nd of each month for 
  the usage of the past month.                                                  
                                                                                
  How can I set up prepaid billing?                                             
  Prepaid billing allows API users to pre-purchase usage credits, which are     
  applied to their monthly invoice. API usage is first deducted from prepaid    
  credits, and any usage exceeding the purchased credits is billed separately.  
  Prepaid billing helps developers plan upfront, providing greater              
  predictability for budgeting and spend management.                            
                                                                                
  Setting up prepaid billing. To prepay and add a credit balance, you must have 
  a payment method on file. Navigate to the Billing section under Organization  
  in the navigation sidebar. In the Overview tab, click Add to credit balance   
  to open the Configure Payment modal.                                          
                                                                                
  Add to credit balance                                                         
  Enter the amount you want to prepay in the Initial credit purchase field.     
  Optionally, you can enable automatic recharge and specify an amount to        
  restore your credit balance when it falls below a set threshold.              
                                                                                
  Configure payment                                                             
  Service Credit Terms and Expiration. Please note that any purchased credits   
  will expire after 1 year, and they are non-refundable.                        
                                                                                
  Others                                                                        
  What are your policies in regard to protecting customer data?                 
  For Voyage-hosted model API endpoints, customers can opt-out from Voyage      
  storing and using their data for future model training so that there is a     
  zero-day retention of the data.                                               
                                                                                
  To opt out, you must have a payment method on file and be an organization     
  Admin. Log into your account dashboard and navigate to the Terms of Service   
  section under Organization in the navigation sidebar. Scroll to the bottom    
  and toggle the Opted In slider to Opted Out.                                  
                                                                                
  Opt out                                                                       
  🚧                                                                            
  You won’t be able to opt-in again in the dashboard after you opt out. If you  
  are interested in opting-in again after opting-out, please contact            
  legal@voyageai.com.                                                           
                                                                                
  Is fine-tuning available?                                                     
  Currently we offer fine-tuned embeddings through subscription. Please email   
  Tengyu Ma (CEO) at tma@voyageai.com if you are interested.                    
                                                                                
  How to contact us?                                                            
  Please email us at contact@voyageai.com for inquiries and customer support.   
                                                                                
  How to get updates from Voyage?                                               
  Follow us on twitter and/or linkedin for more updates!                        
                                                                                
  To subscribe to our newsletter, feel free to send us an email at              
  contact@voyageai.com.

● Committed (8003c0b2). Now your two questions:

  "Did we embed with law?"

  Not in the current code — but there's a stale trace worth chasing 
  operationally.

  - Live default is voyage-4: voyage.rs:50–52 reads COGNITIVE_MCP_VOYAGE_MODEL /
  VOYAGE_MODEL, falling back to "voyage-4". There are zero voyage-law-2
  references in any .rs file.
  - The only law trace is a stale audit doc:
  crates/audits/op-cognitive-mcp/11_11_Configuration_Feature_Flags.md:17 says
  VOYAGE_MODEL "defaults to voyage-law-2" and cites voyage.rs:38. The real
  default moved to line 50–52 and is now voyage-4. So that doc reflects an older
  state where the default was voyage-law-2.
  - Implication: if any embedding ran during that earlier window (default =
  legal model) and produced vectors that were not part of your re-vectorization
  to voyage-4, those would be voyage-law-2 vectors sitting in a collection 
  queried with voyage-4 — a different embedding space, so they'd silently
  mis-rank. The code can't tell me whether such vectors still exist in Qdrant;
  that's an operational check on the live collections. Your re-vectorize to
  voyage-4 should have superseded them, but it's worth confirming no law-2-era
  collection lingers.

  Want me to (a) fix the stale audit doc to say voyage-4, and (b) note the
  "verify no voyage-law-2-era vectors remain in Qdrant" check in SIGNALS?

  "Are we inline with the FAQ?"

  Yes — with the one nano correction now applied:

  - ✅ "all 4 series compatible" — captured; and I corrected nano: it's in the
  space, just capped at ≤512, so excluded from the 1024 collection specifically
  (not from the space).
  - ✅ Domain models separate — voyage-law-2 (legal), voyage-code-3 (code),
  voyage-finance-2 (finance) all treated as own-space; matches "use the domain
  model for that domain," and none are in the trio.
  - ✅ Cosine — Voyage vectors are unit-normalized → cosine = dot-product; our
  Qdrant collection is Cosine. Aligned.
  - ✅ Matryoshka + quantization — now first-class (MatryoshkaDim
  {256/512/1024/2048}, OutputDtype {float/int8/uint8/binary/ubinary}).
  - ⚠️ One gap the FAQ raises that we don't model yet: input_type (query vs
  document). The FAQ says do not omit it for RAG — it prepends retrieval prompts
  and improves quality (embeddings with/without remain compatible, so it's a
  quality knob, not a space knob). It's a call-time parameter, not currently in
  the config. Want me to add an input_type policy field too while we're here?

  "solo" collection kept in a separate retrieval profile — which is exactly

  changes:

  Searched for 1 pattern, read 1 file (ctrl+o to expand)

● Now I see the architecture. Context-aware coding deliberately kept Rust in a 
  separate collection/retrieval profile because it was voyage-code-3 (a
  different space that couldn't fuse). Specifically:

  - default_collections_for_mode() fuses the 6 voyage-4-lite collections (c_cpp,
  go, java, python, typescript, specs_docs) for Completion/Search — Rust is not
  in that list.
  - Rust is the solo rust_lang_rust_lsp_voyage_code_3 collection, queried
  separately via default_rust_collection_from_env().

  Now that you re-vectorized Rust to voyage-4, that separation is the thing to
  undo: Rust is in the shared space and should fuse with the other voyage-4
  collections. Let me confirm how the solo Rust collection is consumed by the
  tools before proposing the exact change:

  Searched for 3 patterns, read 2 files (ctrl+o to expand)

● Now it's fully clear — and there's a real finding: the Rust corpus isn't 
  actually being searched by context-aware coding at all right now.

  collections_from() returns profile.collections = the 6 voyage-4-lite 
  collections (c_cpp, go, java, python, typescript, specs_docs). The Rust
  collection (rust_lang_rust_lsp_voyage_code_3) lives in a separate
  profile.rust_collection field that's surfaced in the JSON output but never 
  added to the fused query. That was correct when Rust was voyage-code-3 (a
  different space that couldn't fuse) — but now that you've re-vectorized Rust
  to voyage-4, it should join the fused set, and right now it's effectively
  orphaned.

  So the fix for context-aware coding is concrete:

  1. Add the (now voyage-4) Rust collection to DEFAULT_VOYAGE4_LITE_COLLECTIONS
  so code_search/code_context actually retrieve Rust alongside the other
  languages.
  2. Rename the constant off …voyage_code_3.
  3. Retire the solo path (DEFAULT_RUST_SOLO_COLLECTION /
  default_rust_collection_from_env / the separate rust_collection field) — Rust
  just fuses now.
  4. Fix the stale comments (lines 296–300: drop "voyage-code-3 best for code /
  keep in a separate retrieval profile" — code is in the voyage-4 shared space
  now).

  The one thing I can't determine and won't guess (a wrong collection name =
  silent empty retrieval): what did you name the re-vectorized voyage-4 Rust 
  collection in Qdrant? The other six follow repos_lsp_<lang>_voyage_4_lite — is
  it repos_lsp_rust_voyage_4_lite, or something else?

  Two questions:
  1. What's the canonical name of the voyage-4 Rust collection?
  2. Fuse it into the default search set (recommended — so code_context picks up
  Rust automatically), or keep Rust selectable but separate?

  Give me the name + intent and I'll make the edit (it builds in
  op-cognitive-mcp, which is downstream of op-plugins, so no cycle concerns
  there).

  crates/op-cognitive-mcp/src/bin/op-cog-admin.rs). And Cozo's persistent
  backend is single-process sled. Let me see what op-cog-admin can already do,

  Ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

● Now the picture is complete. Here's the synthesis and the one architectural
  decision that gates it.

  Why Cozo can't be a Python side-effect

  - The Python vectorizers only write Qdrant (control_refs: [], no Cozo).
  - Cozo is Rust-only (CozoGraphShuttle) and its persistent backend is
  single-process sled — a Python process can't co-write while a Rust process
  holds it.
  - op-cog-admin (the Cozo CLI) currently only does UserAdd/UserList — no corpus
  ingestion.

  So "vectorize and simultaneously populate Cozo, in one pass" forces the
  dual-write into a single Rust ingestion process:

  per chunk:  embed (Voyage) ──► Qdrant upsert  { subid, control_refs
  (canonical), oscal_props }
                            └──► Cozo writes    register_subid(subid) ;
  store_node(chunk/symbol) ;
                                                store_edge(chunk ──maps_to──►
  control) ;
                                                store_compliance_rule(...) for
  compliance content

  The canonical control mappings are the shared key: the same control_refs
  become Qdrant payload and Cozo graph_edges (chunk/rule → OSCAL control), so
  vector search and graph queries agree. That's exactly the empty control_refs: 
  [] we need to fill.

  This means porting the ingestion (chunking + Voyage + dual-write) into Rust —
  the existing rag-ingest/op-cog-admin binaries are the home — rather than
  extending the Python scripts. It's the right foundation and the only way to
  get true simultaneous Cozo population.

  The one thing I need from you

  Where do the canonical control mappings come from? Options I can see:
  - Derive from ComplianceAsCode/content itself (its rule YAML carries
  references: → NIST 800-53 / CCE / STIG IDs).
  - Pull from a prepared map file (is there an oscal-subids-report.md /
  control-map the scripts already reference? vectorize_rust_lang_rust.py:392
  mentions operation-dbus-proto/oscal-subids-report.md).
  - From the oscal_subid_registry plugin / Cozo subid_registry as the authority.

  Tell me the canonical-map source and I'll start building the Rust dual-write 
  ingestion (op-dbus-proto + compliance, Qdrant + Cozo in one pass). I can build

✻ Churned for 1m 10s

❯ so the templates will be useful for industry specific workflow detection.     
  does that change the embedding process?                                       

● Good question — and the answer is: the embedding model doesn't change, but the
  embedding process does for the templates specifically. The voyage-4 trio /
  shared space / Matryoshka all stay. What changes is chunking + payload + how
  Cozo models them, because "industry-specific workflow detection" is a
  filter-and-match problem, not a different vector space.

  Three process changes, scoped to the templates (not the bulk OSCAL spec text):

  1. Chunking — template-aware, not fixed windows. The bulk corpus uses 80-line
  character windows. A template is a coherent unit (a policy template, a TechOps
  doc, a workflow). For detection you want a hit to be a whole usable workflow,
  so chunk per template / per section, not arbitrary windows. Different chunker
  for the templates section.
  2. Payload — add the industry/workflow dimensions. This is the actual enabler.
  Template chunks need:
    - industry / sector (the "industry-specific" filter axis)
    - workflow_type
    - framework + control_refs (already planned)
  So detection = semantic match filtered by industry (Qdrant supports filtered
  search; the pipeline already builds keyword payload indexes). Same vector,
  richer payload.
  3. Cozo — model templates as workflow nodes. store_node(workflow/template) +
  edges template —applies_to→ industry, template —enforces→ control, template 
  —is→ workflow_type. Then "what workflows apply to industry X?" is a graph
  query that hands off to vector search — exactly the detection you're
  describing. Ties into the existing workflows plugin + the subid taxonomy (a
  workflow facet).

  So the corpus splits into two ingestion profiles sharing one embedding model:
  - OSCAL/GDPR/Rego spec text → window chunking, control/subid payload.
  - Templates → unit chunking, +industry/workflow payload, workflow nodes in
  Cozo.

❯ i thought you said that context-4 is not cross compatible                     

● You're right to flag it — and I should correct my recommendation, not just
  defend it.

  Both statements are true and not literally contradictory: voyage-context-4 is
  a separate space (not cross-compatible with the voyage-4 trio), and I proposed
  it as its own collection — which is the only valid way to use a
  non-compatible model. So it's self-consistent.

  But your instinct catches the real cost, and it outweighs the upside. If
  compliance lives on context-4, then:
  - Compliance vectors cannot vector-fuse with the voyage-4 code (op-dbus-proto
  + the repos_lsp_* corpus). You could never do one fused semantic search
  spanning "this code ↔ relevant compliance control."
  - That directly undercuts the thing we're building: control_refs linking
  compliance to the codebase.

  The nuance: the Cozo graph linkage survives either way — control_refs edges
  and subid joins are symbolic, so code↔control↔compliance graph traversal works
  across vector spaces. It's only vector-level cross-domain fusion that
  context-4 breaks. But that fusion is exactly the payoff of putting everything
  in one space.

  So: put compliance on voyage-4, same as the code. One shared space, compliance
  and code fuse, and you've got ~400M free voyage-4 tokens anyway — budget
  isn't the constraint. context-4 would only make sense if compliance were a
  hermetic domain you never cross-reference with code, which is the opposite of
  the control-to-code mapping goal.

  Revised allocation (dropping context-4):

✻ Sautéed for 2m 49s

❯ what is offline  parsers?                                                     

● Sorry — jargon. By "offline parser" I just mean plain code that reads the 
  local files and pulls structured data out of them, with no network or live 
  service involved — no Qdrant connection, no Voyage API, no internet, no token
  cost. Pure text-in → structs-out functions.

  Concretely, the two I meant:
  1. Crosswalk parser — reads compliance-corpora-knowledge.md §5.4 and turns
  those markdown tables into in-memory data: cp-access-mfa → [HIPAA 
  164.308(a)(3)(i), PCI 8.1-8.3, NIST PR.AC-1, …].
  2. OSCAL catalog parser — reads oscal-specs.md and produces control-definition
  records (AC-3, its family, statement text).

  I called them "offline" to contrast with the parts of the pipeline that do
  need live services and can't run from here:
  - embedding → needs the Voyage API (and burns tokens)
  - upsert / set_payload / scroll → needs Qdrant actually running (it was down
  on Jun 21)

  The parsers depend on none of that — they're deterministic functions over
  files I already have, so I can write and unit-test them right now and have the
  canonical-mapping + control-catalog logic verified before you ever spin up
  Qdrant or spend a token. They're the safe foundation; the
  embedding/Qdrant/Cozo-write steps consume their output later when the services
  are up.

  So "build the offline parsers first" = build and prove the data-extraction
  layer now, with nothing external required. Want me to go ahead (clone the
  recovered + industry repos, then build + test those two parsers)?

✻ Baked for 18s

❯ that step saves tokens and output is richer?                                  

● Both yes — but let me be precise about why, so I'm not overselling it.

  Token savings — the parsers themselves cost $0 (they're just reading files),
  but they're what lets us avoid re-embedding:
  - The canonical control_refs, control nodes, and crosswalk edges are symbolic 
  metadata — embeddings never produce them anyway. So you don't gain them by
  re-vectorizing; you gain them by parsing.
  - That unlocks the enrich-without-re-embed path: for compliance content
  already in Qdrant, set_payload the parsed control_refs in place (free), and
  backfill Cozo by scrolling existing Qdrant payloads (free). No tokens spent.
  - The only token cost stays the one-time embed of genuinely new text
  (op-dbus-proto + the compliance docs). Everything already vectorized is
  reused.

  So the saving isn't "the parser saves tokens" — it's "the parser makes the
  metadata layer free, so you never burn tokens re-embedding just to attach
  mappings." Without it you'd be tempted to re-vectorize to "add controls,"
  which would be pure waste.

  Richer output — distinctly, and at zero token cost:
  - Each chunk gets canonical control_refs → you can filter/search by control
  (AC-3, PCI 8.1, 164.308(a)), not just by semantic similarity.
  - Cozo gets a real control graph: control definitions (from OSCAL) +
  rule→control edges + the 143-procedure × 10-framework crosswalk → exact, 
  traversable queries ("everything mapping to AC-3", "PCI 8.1 ↔ which HIPAA/ISO
  controls").
  - Net: you layer exact/symbolic graph retrieval on top of fuzzy/vector
  retrieval over the same corpus — same embeddings, two query modes.

  So: embed once (new content only), then parse + attach + graph for free, and
  the result answers both "what's similar?" and "what maps to this exact
  control?" — which vectors alone can't.

  Want me to start: clone the recovered + industry repos, then build and
  unit-test the two parsers?

    - TechShieldOlamide/trustgrid-compliance-templates
  (fintech/lending/ecommerce), vaibhavjain2608/compliance-policy-templates
  (SOC2/HIPAA/ISO), Auxin-io/cybersecurity-templates — the industry-declared
  template repos (industry comes verbatim from the repo, never inferred)
  2. Stages the in-tree oscal-specs.md + compliance-corpora-knowledge.md as a
  local repo, and symlinks operation-dbus-proto.
  3. Vectorizes with voyage-4 / dim 1024 / input_type=document into two
  collections:
    - compliance_voyage_4 (20M file cap → includes the 14M oscal-specs.md)
    - op_dbus_proto_voyage_4 (2M cap → excludes oscal-specs.md so it isn't
  double-embedded)
    - hand-set budget: VOYAGE_API_KEY_RUST:150M, VOYAGE_API_KEY_LITE:150M,
  resumable, stops cleanly on budget (exit 75) or if Qdrant is down (the Jun-21
  failure — it preflights localhost:6333).

  To run

  ! bash ~/git/scripts/ingest_compliance_opdbus.sh
  (Override if needed: KEY_ENVS=… BATCH=… QDRANT_URL=… bash ….) Needs Qdrant up
  + ~/.bash_secrets keys.

  What it intentionally leaves out (the free, no-token pass)

  Cozo population + canonical control_refs from the §5.4 crosswalk + OSCAL
  control nodes. Those are symbolic — embeddings don't produce them — so they
  cost zero tokens and run as a separate enrich pass (Qdrant scroll → Cozo,

✻ Cogitated for 4m 6s

❯ should that be done after all embedding is finished?                          

● Mostly yes — and there's a concrete reason, not just tidiness.

  The enrich pass that writes onto Qdrant points (control_refs set_payload, Cozo
  backfill-from-scroll) should run after a collection's embedding is finished —
  because the embedding upsert writes control_refs: []. If you enrich a
  collection and then the embedding resumes and re-upserts any of those points,
  it would clobber your canonical control_refs back to empty. Running enrich
  after a collection is complete avoids that entirely. (The pipeline is
  resume-safe via done-files, so it only re-upserts repos it hasn't finished —
  but "enrich after done" sidesteps the race cleanly.)

  But "after all embedding" is stronger than needed — it's per-collection and 
  idempotent, so:

  ┌──────────────────────────────────────┬──────────────────┬───────────────┐
  │                 Step                 │ When it can run  │  Depends on   │
  │                                      │                  │  embedding?   │
  ├──────────────────────────────────────┼──────────────────┼───────────────┤
  │ Offline parsers (crosswalk + OSCAL   │ now — pure,      │ no            │
  │ catalog → in-memory map)             │ offline          │               │
  ├──────────────────────────────────────┼──────────────────┼───────────────┤
  │ Cozo backfill of already-vectorized  │                  │               │
  │ collections (repos_lsp_*,            │ now (Qdrant up)  │ already done  │
  │ specs_docs)                          │                  │               │
  ├──────────────────────────────────────┼──────────────────┼───────────────┤
  │ control_refs set_payload + Cozo on   │ after the        │ yes — that    │
  │ compliance_voyage_4                  │ compliance embed │ collection    │
  │                                      │  finishes        │               │
  ├──────────────────────────────────────┼──────────────────┼───────────────┤
  │ same on op_dbus_proto_voyage_4       │ after op-dbus    │ yes — that    │
  │                                      │ embed finishes   │ collection    │
  └──────────────────────────────────────┴──────────────────┴───────────────┘

  So: build the tool now, backfill the existing corpus anytime, and gate only
  the new collections' enrich on their own embed completing. It's safe to re-run
  (upserts/overwrites are idempotent), so the simplest operational choice is
  one enrich run after the ingest script finishes — but you're not blocked from
  populating Cozo from the already-vectorized data immediately.

  Want me to build the Rust enrich/backfill tool now (it's all offline +
  Qdrant-side, zero Voyage tokens), so it's ready the moment the embed run
  completes?

  ⎿  Interrupted · What should Claude do instead?

❯ make sure to check for already embedded, keep in mind that the already        
  embedded affects remaininng token count                                       

● Critical points, both handled by design:
  - Already-embedded check: the pipeline writes {collection}.{repo}.done files
  and skips them (--skip-existing default on) — so no repo is re-embedded, no
  tokens re-spent. I'll also add a preflight that reports the done-count per 
  collection so you see what's already done before it spends anything.
  - Prior usage affects remaining: I set each tier's budget cap to the current 
  remaining free balance from your paste (already net of prior spend — e.g.
  large/LITE = 69M, not 200M), using fresh budget files so stale used counts
  from the old runs don't mis-account. Cap = real free remaining ⇒ the run can't
  exceed what's actually left.

  Rewriting the script to cover all remaining + new corpora with model/key
  rotation:

❯ this includes cozo write?                                                     

● Two things — first your question, then a real issue the preflight just
  exposed.

  "This includes Cozo write?"

  No. This script is embed → Qdrant only. The Python pipeline (vectorize_lsp.py)
  writes vectors + payload to Qdrant with control_refs: [] and never touches 
  Cozo — it can't, Cozo is Rust + single-process sled.

  Cozo population + canonical control_refs is the separate, free, no-token Rust 
  enrich pass (offline parsers + Qdrant scroll → Cozo + set_payload). That's
  deliberate, for the reasons we established:
  - Cozo facts are symbolic — embeddings don't produce them, so re-running the
  embed wouldn't add them anyway.
  - Running it after the embed avoids the resume-clobber race (a re-upsert
  resetting control_refs to []).
  - It costs zero Voyage tokens.

  So "simultaneously populate Cozo" resolved to: embed once (paid) here → enrich
  Cozo for free right after. If you want, I can chain the enrich tool to
  auto-run at the end of this script — but the Cozo write itself is the Rust
  tool I haven't built yet, not this script.

  Issue: collection names don't match (affects your "already embedded" check)

  The done-files reveal inconsistent historical collection names:
  - rust → repos_lsp_rust_voyage_4_large (46 done) ✅ matches my script
  - java → lsp_java (2 done), specs_docs → specs_docs (14 done, incl
  ComplianceAsCode__content, usnistgov__OSCAL)

  …but my script (and the retrieval code's DEFAULT_VOYAGE4_COLLECTIONS) uses
  repos_lsp_java_voyage_4_lite / repos_specs_docs_voyage_4_lite. Those show 
  done=0 — so as written, the script would re-embed already-done java/specs_docs
  content into a different collection (wasted tokens + split data). Exactly the
  re-spend you told me to avoid.

  I can't resolve it from here because Qdrant isn't reachable (so I can't see
  the real live collection names). Two ways forward:
  1. You run curl -s localhost:6333/collections and paste it — I pin the exact
  existing names.
  2. I make the script auto-discover at runtime: query /collections, and for
  each group match the existing collection (by the done-file prefix / a
  substring), only creating new ones for compliance + op-dbus.

  I'd recommend (2) — the script self-corrects on your machine where Qdrant is

❯ why is plugins service readonly and the projected tree not working?           

● Both questions have the same root cause — it's working as designed, and the
  projection is telling you Qdrant is down. (Also: my second command erred on a
  shell-quoting bug, not the bus — ignore that.)

  Why the plugins service is read-only

  It's a projection (the read side of a CQRS split), not the write path.
  org.opdbus.v1.plugins is served by projection_serv and every node exposes only
  the org.opdbus.v1.plugins.ProjectedObject interface — Data / EntityId / 
  EntityType / State properties. It's a read-only mirror of /dev/shm 
  present-state (the [[project_shm_reactive_principle]]: components read SHM;
  they don't poll/mutate it).

  Writes deliberately do not go through this service. They go through the
  MutationEngine (gRPC PluginService.CallMethod) — the single mutation authority
  where every mutation passes the Ghostbridge interceptor gate. So: reads = 
  D-Bus projection, writes = MutationEngine gRPC. The projection is read-only on
  purpose so nothing mutates state
  ⎿  Interrupted · What should Claude do instead?

  ┌─────────────────────┬─────────────────────────────────┬──────────────────┐
  │                     │       netmaker (STOPPED)        │   netmaker-ui    │
  │                     │                                 │    (RUNNING)     │

  ├─────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────┼──────────────────┤

  │ security.privileged │ true (idmap [])                 │ unprivileged     │
  │                     │                                 │ (real idmap)     │
  └─────────────────────┴─────────────────────────────────┴──────────────────┘

  So the /run tmpfs EINVAL tracks with netmaker being privileged — a privileged
  OCI container's /run tmpfs mount fails where the unprivileged one succeeds.
  The socket-under-/run is identical on both and isn't the trigger (ui proves
  it).

  So there are really two separate things, and your instinct points at the fix
  for one of them:
  1. Provision netmaker's socket the right way — run createunixsocket for it
  (via the plugin method / zbusctl createsocket --name netmaker --port 8081,1883
  --path /run/ghostbridge/container.sock), same as the others should be. This
  is correct regardless.
  2. The start-crash is most likely the security.privileged: true on the OCI
  container, not the socket. netmaker-ui (unprivileged) starts; netmaker
  (privileged) hits the /run EINVAL.

  So before we declare the socket the cause: do you want netmaker to be
  privileged? If it doesn't truly need it (ui doesn't), dropping
  security.privileged likely lets /run mount and the container start — then we
  run createunixsocket for it on top. Want me to (a) run the createunixsocket
  provisioning for netmaker, and/or (b) test the start with security.privileged

❯ bigger question is shold everything be on session bus since that is what is   
  identiy gated                                                                 

● That's the right question to be asking, and it cuts deeper than the socket
  bug. Let me separate the principle from the current breakage.

  The principle: yes — one identity-gated surface

  Right now there are two doors:
  - System bus → org.opdbus.v1.plugins (projection, reads) — gated only by D-Bus
  policy (uid/peer), not by the Ghostbridge footprint.
  - Session bus (/run/op-dbus/session-bus.sock) → org.opdbus.CognitiveMcp →
  MutationEngine (writes) — this is where the wristband/footprint is actually
  enforced.

  So today the write path is footprint-gated and the read path isn't. Per
  [[project_zero_trust_principle]], the wristband is supposed to be the only
  gate — "IP ACLs, firewall rules, port restrictions are theater." An ungated
  read surface that anything on the box can scrape is the same kind of theater
  hole: it leaks present-state (plugin configs, identities, topology) without
  ever presenting a footprint. So if a consumer crosses the trust boundary, it 
  should come through the identity-gated surface — reads included. That argues
  for consolidating onto the gated (session/cognitive) door, exactly as you're
  saying.

  The one legitimate exception (scope it tightly)

  The identity gate is enforced at ingress by the interceptor. A system-bus
  projection is acceptable only for consumers that are provably same-host, 
  already-behind-the-gate, and trusted (e.g. projection_serv feeding op-web
  locally, which itself sits behind xray/xraqy). The test is concrete: does any 
  untrusted or cross-boundary caller read org.opdbus.v1.plugins directly? If yes
  → it must move behind the footprint. If it's only internal plumbing that
  never faces an external peer, system bus is a local convenience, not a hole. I
  won't claim "all reads must move" as a blanket — it depends on who consumes
  the projection, which is checkable.

  So the principled end state: one footprint-gated surface for anything crossing
  the boundary (reads served through the same gate as writes), and the system
  bus demoted to verified-internal-only — or removed.

  But note: the gated door is itself broken right now

  The session-bus mutation backend just gave BrokenPipe —
  org.opdbus.CognitiveMcp accepted the connection and dropped the call. So even
  the correct gated path isn't serving createsocket at the moment. That's an
  orthogonal bug from the architecture decision: consolidating everything onto
  the session bus only helps once that backend actually responds.

  Two threads, your call on order:
  1. Architecture: I record this (single footprint-gated surface; audit who
  reads the system-bus projection; plan to move reads behind the gate) — want it
  in SIGNALS as a decision/concern?
  2. Unblock now: debug why org.opdbus.CognitiveMcp drops the createsocket call
  (its log / whether it even owns the name on that session bus), since that's
  what's stopping the socket provisioning you asked for.

  Which first — or both?

  - The single-gate consolidation is what makes it "as advertised." The system
  is sold on [[project_zero_trust_principle]] — the wristband as the only gate.
  A working write-gate plus a parallel ungated system-bus read surface isn't
  that model; it's "mostly Zero Trust." So to match the advertising, the gate
  has to both work and be the only door across the boundary.

  The one place I won't overstate: whether the consolidation is required vs.
  hardening hinges on a checkable fact — does anything untrusted/cross-boundary 
  actually read org.opdbus.v1.plugins directly? If the only readers are trusted

  consolidation is defense-in-depth. If an external peer can reach it, it's a
  real hole and required. That audit decides it — not a blanket claim.

  So: both, in dependency order. Fix the gated backend first (unblocks
  everything immediately and is unconditional), then audit the projection's
  consumers and consolidate/move reads behind the gate.

  createsocket fails:

  The definitive bug: zbusctl targets names that don't exist

  Three-way mismatch between what zbusctl calls and what the code actually
  serves:

  ┌──────────┬─────────────────────────┬───────────────────────────────────┐
  │          │     zbusctl calls       │       Code actually serves        │
  │          │    (main.rs:163-165)    │    (dbus_interface.rs:3-5,27)     │

  │          │ e                       │ mcp                               │
  ├──────────┼─────────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────────┤

  │ e        │ V1                      │ p                                 │
  └──────────┴─────────────────────────┴───────────────────────────────────┘

  So zbusctl createsocket can never reach the service — ServiceUnknown on the
  system bus, dropped/BrokenPipe on the session bus. zbusctl is stale; it
  predates the .v1.plugins naming. That's the proximate cause.

  Two more layers behind it

  - op-cognitive-mcp isn't on the bus. PID 740 is running but not connected to
  the session bus, so even with the correct name the CognitiveMcp interface
  isn't being served. (And org.opdbus.v1.plugins on the session bus is owned by
  ovs-dbus-init, not cognitive-mcp — so there's a name-ownership question too.)
  - tonic-web reflection (your point) — yes, plausibly the same problem one 
  transport over. The mutation surface also rides the gRPC bridge (tonic-web +
  reflection). If reflection advertises a surface that doesn't match what's
  actually mounted — exactly the drift we fixed in OD-29 with the shared
  route-builder — then reflection-driven callers (and anything discovering
  createunixsocket via reflection) hit unmounted/wrong methods and fail the same
  way the D-Bus name mismatch does. It's a parallel instance of "the advertised
  address ≠ the served address."

  So it's one disease on three surfaces: zbusctl's hardcoded D-Bus names, 
  cognitive-mcp not claiming the name on the bus, and the gRPC reflection 
  surface — all need to point at the one real address 
  (org.opdbus.v1.plugins.CognitiveMcp / the actually-mounted gRPC services).
